ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e217	The Communication of Perspectives should focus on the long-term consequences of today's policy decisions and outline a general policy direction. The notification provides a fairly detailed description of where we stand today, and what developments will affect the Norwegian economy in the long term. Let me give you some examples. change in population and life expectancy labour input and productivity phase-out of petroleum activities in the long term international economy climate change Our starting point is in the message described as well. Norway is a safe and good country to live in. We have succeeded in combining high value creation with small differences. Wealth is more evenly distributed and income differences are less inherited than in most other countries. We have extensive public welfare schemes funded through a fissure layer. The state finances are solid. The combination of good growth capacity, even distribution and public schemes are characteristics of our social model, and are something we should be proud of together. And in addition, we're productive. Norway has had a higher productivity growth than many other countries in the last 20 years. In recent years there have been more «ruskets», I would say, to use a separate expression. Growth in productivity has fallen or declined somewhat since 2006 — increased after the financial crisis — but is now rising again. That's good, because it's an important indicator. The analyses in the Perspective Communication also show that we are facing challenges in other areas in the coming years. We live longer, that's good. Increased life expectancy is a sign of a good society. But it challenges the sustainability of public finances if we don't do the job over the life cycle anymore. If we continue today's work and the welfare arrangements this collapse has decided to put ourselves behind, we need to increase revenue in public budgets or cut other expenditures equivalent to 6 pst. by the value creation of the mainland economy up to 2060. I think there's a lot of consensus in the hall about what challenges lie ahead of us. The debate is more about how we, as a nation, should equip ourselves to meet these in the future. The majority believe that the challenges are best addressed in the framework of the Norwegian model and with a responsible use of oil money. The revenues from petroleum activities are transitory, but could benefit future generations by spending no more than the income from the State pension fund abroad. It is not the primary pension fund to safeguard our well-being in the future, but the value of our joint efforts in the labour market. The high participation rate in employment in Norway compared to Europe is mainly due to more women and older jobs. High levels of gender equality, good maternity leave arrangements, birth rate and full childcare have contributed to women working more than in very many other countries. Although female employment rates are high, there is still a significant potential to facilitate the full-time work of more women who wish to do so. That is why I am pleased that we are doing this work. The government has recently launched a number of new measures to combat involuntary part-time work, including a statutory right to a job share that is in line with how much you actually work. Another potential that can meet the financing needs of the future is to get people to work longer. The number of expected years of retirement for a newborn has increased by nearly nine years since 1967 and until 2011. The reform of pensions, which is behind all the parties on the Stortinget, with the exception of the Progress Party, aims to reverse the trend in which the labour market is getting shorter and shorter. The new pension tax rules introduced in 2011 also make it easier to combine work and pensions. The reform of the labour market and the so-called IA work facilitate the combination of work and social security. Therefore, whether this reform works as intended, let us not be afraid, but let us be inspired, in the sense that the employment effect will significantly reduce the future need for public budgets to be covered. In report No. 22/2011 by the SSB, the reform is estimated to increase the number of hours worked by around 7 pst in 2050. The impact of the reform will, however, be able to reduce the coverage demand of government budgets from 6 p.m. to dry 2 pst. I'm not saying this to paint the situation, but to show that it's the value of people joining the work force -- either that we who are healthy work a little longer than today, or that we get new groups into the working life -- that will be the key to whether we can also say in the future that we have the sustainability of the well-being we grant ourselves. There has been a lot of discussion over the last few weeks on labour immigration to Norway, and it has increased sharply in recent years. I think we must be able to say that many of the missions performed in Norwegian society today could not have been done if we had not had labour immigration. It has been a good thing for Norway in the short term. Some say that immigration to Norway should save the welfare state in the long term. I do not agree with that. It is also clear from the perspective of the Communication that importing labour migrants into Norway is not the solution to the financing challenges in the future. I would say that in this way that labour immigration does not solve or exacerbate the situation in the long term. The report shows that immigrants coming to Norway — when everything other than the labour market is equal — are gradually becoming the Nordics the most. I have just pointed out that by 2050 we have an unmet need for funding, even if you only count on the work of everyone who lives in Norway today. So the answer to this field too is that it has been successful in the short term, but we depend on getting more people to work longer and to stand longer in the workplace, also by immigrants. The last thing I want to point out and underline before I link any comments to the opposition's comments is that in the future we have to do what has made Norway what we are today. We must have an open, competitive market economy at the bottom, with a resilient, well-qualified workforce and a large, comprehensive public sector. It can, no matter how you turn it around, become much more productive of course, but compared to very many other countries it is an efficient public sector, and a sector that is characterized by a high degree of conversion here as well. I mention some reforms in recent years: the quality reform of higher education, the Nav reform, the Cooperation reform and technology projects in many public institutions. We need to do more of this work, but we need to remember that in the public sector, work is the most important input in most areas in which we are involved. There are limits to the speed or effectiveness of care for the elderly in Norway, because it is now about being close and caring and being present for people in their last days. It is also a major concern that – although it is a potential for technology also in the care of the elderly and other sectors, we are not going to avoid it – it is human care that is the important thing. Then it's hard to imagine how much more efficiency you can imagine. I have mentioned some of the ways we can meet the challenges of the future. Then I hope for contributions from the opposition that do not paint the situation today, nor do I black paint how Norway can meet the challenges by 2050. I have registered, and it also says in the setting, that some attempts have been made to gather about some comments and areas. I have to say, I'm not very impressed. It builds up, side up and down, I'd say, with many nice suggestions that you already agree on. It may not be the most difficult thing in life to write about something you already agree on. I have to admit that when I sat down and read the comments when these were presented at the end of the committee procedure, I thought, "Well, I'm sorry, now I think maybe they've manned up to agree on some of the things they disagree on. But when someone comes to that chapter, it's -- yeah, I shouldn't say it, but someone might call it -- a stomachache. Because when you come to the rule of action, whether it applies or not, you don't agree. When it comes to whether climate change is a challenge for Norway and the world, there is no consensus. It is not agreed whether or not immigration is a resource for Norway. It is not even agreed that it is a political objective to ensure small differences and even distribution. We can hope that the debate may help to clarify some of these questions. By doing so, I recommend the financial committee's proposal for a decision in the deadline for the 2013 notification of the perspective.

If you think this is a siding in today's debate, it's not very much a perspective on what the Workers' Party delivers in this debate. I think that's sad. As mentioned, a lot is going well in Norway, and we're going to enjoy it. I've just been to Indonesia. Having Norwegian enterprises investing in business, in value creation, is part of the solution for those countries that are struggling. But there was also a country where the infrastructure is not built up in line with the need, where the cooling costs are enormous, and where an investment in infrastructure rather than capital would undoubtedly improve the growth capacity of the country. When we look forward to this debate, we should not only be glad that things are going well in Norway, we should also look at the robustness of the economic development at the bottom here. Will our children and grandchildren be able to enjoy good viability – as we are doing today? If we don't see why things are going well today, and understand that, you won't be able to really assess what you have to do to make sure that we still have such a situation tomorrow. Many give the impression that the rule of action for the use of oil money is the primary factor in Norway’s success. Let me stress that the Advance Party agrees that we should have a good use of our oil wealth. It's important to make sure that you phase it into the economy, so that you don't just get the prices up. It is important to have a generation perspective on what you do. Therefore, the Progress Party agrees with the other parties on the civil side that there should be a restriction on how much of the oil money can be consumed in the public sector. But we disagree with the rest of the Storting to have a percentage rule that limits investment in socially profitable assets. There is no company in Norway today that would say the following: we have a good project, but we would rather postpone it for a few years, so we don't invest too much in a year. When you have a project that is profitable, you should implement it as quickly as possible – in order to enjoy it as quickly as possible. It has a long-term perspective. Oil money use has increased by about 10 years. 60 B.C.E. kr. Oil investments on Norwegian soil have increased by 120 billion in the last ten years. kr, i.e. twice as much. There is no rule as to how much oil investment can grow. This government spent only 20 days dealing with the Åsta Hansteen project, 55 billion. kr, but trying to spend more money on capital investment in the Norwegian economy via the State budget, it is said to be irresponsible. I don't see that context. There is a difference between spending money on consumption or on investment, but there is no difference whether the money is used to invest in oil or to get away with the press effect in the Norwegian economy. The return varies, it does. A little bit of what makes me worry is that the government doesn't see these differences itself, and thinks that it's the oil money usage of the state budget that has made the wealth possible, but at the same time ignores the fact that municipal debt has increased sharply just under the red-green government. In addition, loans-funded debt or liabilities in private households have also increased sharply. Thus, despite a huge increase in wages and salaries, the debt ratio in Norwegian households is now close to 200 pst. of the average wages and salaries – versus 120 pst. when the government took over. So here we have four factors that have been a huge catalyst into the Norwegian economy, which the government more or less ignores when describing the success – and gives the impression that this is about the rule of action for the use of oil money. I'm concerned when oil investments are nine times larger than investments in the mainland industry. I'm concerned when oil investments have tripled in ten years, while investments in the mainland industry have halved in the last few years. Then we have to look at the industry's framework conditions – make sure we don't feel so rich that we can say as the good Communist Aslak Sira Myhre said: We think we have so much milk in this country that we can shoot the cow. When a gas-based aluminium plant wants to establish itself in Norway but is referred to other countries, something is wrong. When this government has previously wanted to steer young people away from petroleum-related education, what creates values has not been seen. When you've ended up fighting the shipping companies all the way to the Supreme Court, where the government lost, you don't have respect for those who create the values. When one introduces an industrial power system that is so bad that no actors want to use it, but rather goes into the power market – as the government thought was bad itself – it is something wrong. When one gets the highest level of public employment in Europe but is unable to win any gold in McKinsey's unrelenting excellence in public service quality, one also shows that there is an improvement potential. Right then, one should discuss how to invest better in the public sector in order to make those who work there more productive. Rather, the progress will provide doctors, nurses and teachers with better tools so that they can become better and more productive rather than boasting that you have hired more and more people. For us, there is no certainty that the cooperation reform will be resolved by considering fax guards in Norwegian hospitals, or when you look at the NRK, that the Polish police will receive financial support to buy better tools to halve the response time, while the police in Asker and Bærum can only shed tears to ensure that they have not received the same. Thus, there is the possibility of streamlining the public sector without weakening the service provision, but simply making individual employees there more productive, but then one must be able to see the difference between investment and consumption. Similarly, we should become a little more aware of the growth of employment. It is very gratifying that there are more than 300,000 more people working today than ten years ago. The trend shift came in the summer of 2003 during the Bondevik government. Then the number of hours of work started to rise, and from 2004 employment started to rise. The SSB in its report 1/2011, Economic Analysis, showed that oil investments were the main driving force. What worries me is that in the last few years, the employment of the Nordics has started to decline, while there is a continuing growth in the employment of foreigners. There is something wrong in this economy when we disapprove of our own and just replace them with outside labour. It's not the fault of the workers, but it has something to do with how we set up our systems, and I think that both the individual norwegian and Norwegian economies would have benefited from us making sure that more norwegian people come to work. Then we have to take action to do this, and it is described in our remarks. Norway cannot sit passively on the sidelines pending a global carbon price, but actively seek leadership with the rest of Europe in the necessary energy conversion work. To achieve the 2-degree target, large parts of the world's well-known fossil energy resources must remain untouched. It is a major challenge that too many people of pre-employment age are not included in ordinary employment. Developing and attracting the right skills for future needs is a major challenge. The problem is to have a workforce that is constantly adapted to the needs of society and business, which is an increasing problem in several Western economies. We need to train more people properly, and we need to create a working life that is constantly focusing on new learning. It is a prerequisite for Norway's ability to adapt and to compete that we have a high-quality education system from kindergarten to university. Most of the Nordics are becoming less and less fit to work. Let me add another perspective that the perspective communication goes by quickly: the public sector and state enterprises employ about. One million people. Children and young people account for approx. 1.1 million people, and at the base of 1 million, are old-age pensioners. Of a population of 5 million people, only 1.2 million work in the private sector, providing all the income we live on. In the statement of perspective, the government chooses to sub-communicate the most important challenge facing our country, namely immigration from countries that are remote from us culturally, linguistically and religiously. ‘Norway is no less Norwegian by changing’ says author Khalid Salimi in today’s Evening Post. Maybe, maybe not -- it depends of course on how to define 'Norwegian'. Everyone understands and accepts that people from other countries live in Norway. In fact, I have not met one person who wants it not to live in other countries in Norway. The challenge is the pace of this migration and what these changes entail, culturally and economically. Norway has already become very different in just a few decades. In eastern parts of Oslo today, immigrants make up half of the population. Such development and rate of change are not sustainable – not sustainable culturally, not sustainable religiously, nor sustainable economically. But it continues anyway with undiminished strength. That's how the government wants it. Some refer to the emigration of the North to America in the 1800s and 1900s. Yes, one in three Northmen traveled to America in the 1800s and 1900s, and they settled together. They settled in Brooklyn, Minnesota, in Seattle. Yes, they lived together, yes, they married, yes, they spoke Norwegian. The whole point of the American melting pot was that in the next generation they would have a slightly wider perspective. Then they might marry a Dutchman or a Chilean – one from another nation. But it doesn't happen here, because the government has said it's quite okay how today's immigrants do it. An asylum seeker is granted refugee status for on average three relatives of Norway through family reunification. Spouse is almost without exception in the country of origin. It also happens in the second generation. In 2012, 15 400 non-Western immigrants arrived in Norway. It is similar to that of the population in the municipality of Henefoss or in the municipality of Rällingen. This happens every year. That's the perspective. The Seg Government is speeding up, for it is not that ethnic nordics and immigrants are increasingly united in a large happy family. No, in the Grorud valley, in fact, those who are from the same nation are not moving anything particularly outside the group that is from the same nation. Afghans are associated to a small extent with Pakistani people, etc. There's actually a fight between them as well. This is again a wild policy, a policy of which the government boasts. You look with open eyes at what's going on, but you keep doing this. Many Nordics are dismayed by developments. They feel alienated in their own neighborhood. They're not being heard, they're fighting windmills. No, it doesn't matter. The challenges are growing, and the Nordics must repel in more and more areas. Of course, the disclosures of the financial paper over the past few weeks should have received the attention of all media. Instead, it's a deafening silence. The newspaper made a poor attempt to contradict the calculations, but even an exercise in political correctness they had to embark on. The numbers' speech is ready. Demographic developments in Norway are taking place at a record pace, and the government is increasing. The perspective statement says: ‘We cannot continue over time a welfare system where more and more receive more than they contribute.’ No, and today's immigration policy is just contributing to this. For each year this policy continues, public welfare sustainability is reduced. What does the government do with that? Nothing. Various figures for the government parties even claim that today’s policy is profitable. The SSB statistics recently showed that while 70 pst. of non-immigrants – i.e. the Nordics – the same figures show that of those with Somali background is 31 pst., of those with Eritrean background is 40 pst., of those with Iraqi background is 42 pst., of those with Afghan background 45 pst., of those with Pakistani background 47 pst. These figures are probably due to different ratios. But it is absolutely impossible to ignore the fact that there is also an economic motivation here, and that various social security schemes contribute to passivity. Senior researcher Knut Røed at the Frischsenteret tells the Finanzavisen that some people get into the labour market badly, but well into the welfare system. He also says that the scheme is beneficial for people with low income potential and many children. The immigrant groups, who, despite up to 20 years of residence in Norway, are a challenge in terms of employment, come from Somalia, Eritrea, Iraq, Afghanistan, Pakistan, Morocco and Turkey. In total, these represent 148 000 people — several of them with the lowest employment rates. Both immigration and birth surpluses in the lowest-employment groups were twice as high as in the highest-employment groups. When people in this room talk about immigration, I wonder what they're really talking about, because they divide the immigrants into those who're going to work, and those who're not going to work. For me, all immigrants to Norway are going to work. When a division artificially does this, it also artificially separates on an ethnic basis. When we say that those who come from far away – those who come as asylum seekers and reunite – it is a little too bad, while those who come from Poland, Lithuania, etc., have to paint and stand on our houses. It's kind of a worry that they stop doing it. But the concern we should have is, of course, that many of the 15,400 we get on average every year, not working, nor being integrated into Norwegian society. Both immigration and birth surpluses in the lowest-employment groups — i.e. those from the countries I mentioned — were twice as high as in the high-employment groups. A perspective message that does not mention the greatest sovereign challenge for the welfare society is pointless. I can add: it's simply cowardly. It's cowardly, because these are challenges you can choose to deal with, or you can let development continue until our saved oil revenues can no longer bear the economic challenge. The costs of today's immigration policy mean that each taxpayer pays, on average, NOK 14 000 per year, i.e. NOK 28 000 for each household where both are at work. I repeat what is in the perspective message: ‘Tax and social security systems must therefore be designed to pay for their work.’ Well, then it's time to establish a relationship between rhetoric and politics.

In this regard, we still believe that concession is correct, and in practice no great burden on those concerned. ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e1358	I see that there are some dilemmas in this case, where we are going to guarantee copyright, but also internet users. The Breathwork Act is in no way new, and there has been broad agreement on it, among other things. to give authors the rights to their works and performance. The problem now is the technological development and sharing culture available on the Internet. But copyright also applies here, although the awareness of this may seem relatively low. The best thing would perhaps be to stimulate the development of new services for the downloading and streaming of copyrighted material. But now I have chosen to be pragmatic and to help ensure that rightholders who get their copyright violated on the Internet, i.e. are deprived of their works, must be given the opportunity to exercise their rights. We have to recognise that artists and authors should be able to live from their work, and then we have to ensure the enforcement of copyright. More and more people expect to access most of the information and media products on the Internet. And of course, it is absolutely essential that attractive and legal services are developed in order to provide access to, and thus reduce, the disadvantages. Legal services must be perceived as good. They must be able to compete for ease of use, selection and time of launch. Fortunately, we see a lot going on in the film and television series offer and, not least, in the field of music. But there is still a great potential for development, especially in film, games and books services. And it is now difficult to imagine that legal services alone can sufficiently reduce illegal use. Suffering from the development of good legal services, it will be difficult to compete with what is unlawfully offered free of charge. An example is the launch of the movie "Kon-Tiki". The day after the launch on DVD, iTunes, and Get Box, it was available at The Pirate Bay. And despite good legal alternatives, it was a huge division. For the time being, enforcement means are being assumed to contribute to a satisfactory reduction in the illegal activity. However, there is no contradiction between the need to stimulate parallel development and investment in new legal services. The purpose of the case now is to strengthen the rightholders' ability to enforce their rights in the event of Internet violations. The draft legislation focuses on finding balanced solutions to safeguard privacy, legal certainty and freedom of expression. Like others here, I would like to make it clear that no measures are proposed that could lead to the exclusion of individuals from the Internet. Initially, I believe that enforcement of copyright on the Internet should be a police task. But now it is the case that the Supreme Court has rejected the fact that only the police are supposed to be able to enforce copyright on the Internet. The Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on the Law on Civil Matters should be enforceable by those who have their rights violated. This was established by the Supreme Court in the Max Manus case. Civil law enforcement is the main track of the law, cf. enforcement of ‘analogue’ copyright. The proposals promoted are to a large extent a clarification of the current law or, as the Data Protection Authority writes, ‘changes in the copyright law that do not result in major changes in reality in the current legal state’. One of the proposals is that today's concession requirements for IP addresses registration are terminated, but otherwise the Personal Data Act shall apply. Furthermore, a duty to notify the Data Protection Authority will continue, which may control the way in which the processing is carried out. The Data Protection Authority makes an assessment of the purpose of the registration and thus helps to determine whether you have real reasons to register the addresses. It is essential that only the IP address used for the infringement be accessed by registration, not the identity behind it. In order that the identity may be disclosed to the rightholder, a court decision imposing extradition shall be required. The postal and telecommunications supervision shall also assess the issue. So there are several elements that guarantee privacy. It is also important that a balance of interests be made in the decision making process, i.e.: that the reasons justifying extradition are required to outweigh the grounds of professional secrecy. The understanding of copyright changes over time. Today's legal text, both what I have touched on in my post, and the other paragraphs, will certainly be subject to new changes, as evolution progresses. There will probably be more debates about the awareness of copyright and copyright, how it should be understood and how it should be ensured in an ever-growing divisional culture.

That's why today we depend in a completely different way on an acceptance that we don't steal because it's wrong – and not just that we don't steal because we think it's embarrassing to be discovered. Both the legitimacy of the legislation and the conclusion of the values at the bottom mean that we agree jointly on the rules of the game that have been laid down and that we agree on the basic value that not least respect for the property of others. It is rooted in our culture, in a long historical Christian tradition, where the seventh commandment reads: ‘You shall not steal.’ Respect for this is built-in from generation to generation and today appears to be a fundamental value in our society. ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e1425	New technologies primarily provide opportunities for artistry, innovation and dissemination. The proposal for changes to copyright provides legal entities with better tools to enforce copyright in the event of serious Internet infringements. The legislative proposal has three main parts. The first proposal concerns the possibility of proving that there has been an infringement. The proposal assumes that legal entities are given access to register IP addresses that are useful to copyright restrictions, and it is important to be able to provide evidence that copyright rights are violated. Registration of IP addresses for this purpose is already allowed today, but it requires a concession from the Data Protection Authority. I feel that during the debate there have been critical remarks relating to this very element, i.e. that we remove the requirement of concession. I would therefore like to stress that, of course, we keep the other rules of the Personal Information Act, and they still apply to how this type of information should be handled. Even if the concession requirement is removed, the type of activity will continue to impose a reporting obligation on the Data Protection Authority, and it also allows the Data Protection Supervisor to supervise who benefits from the home and how the activity is carried out. The second proposal regulates access to the identity of an IP address that is used for copyright impairment. The legal entities shall be able to obtain information through the Court of Justice as to what Internet subscriptions may be attached to the infringement referred to in this paragraph. I note that the representative from the Left says that this violates our legal principle. In that case, the Left believes that the Supreme Court's reasoning violates our rule of law, because the Supreme Court has already stated that the courts can provide this type of subscription information in accordance with the rules of dispute. The bill follows up on this and implies a clarification of this access. The threshold for the provision of this type of information will not be reduced relative to the current one. Thirdly, it is suggested that courts should be able to require Internet service providers to prevent or impede access to websites where copyright is infringed in a serious case. I also record that the representative from the Left says that this is similar to something that a country does not like to compare itself with. It's new to me that we don't like to compare ourselves with Sweden, which is an example of a country where one practices similar instruments. It's a powerful tool, so the threshold for using this will be very high. These must be websites which contain a large amount of material that clearly infringes copyright law. If these conditions are met, the Court of Justice shall exercise a broad margin of interest to assess whether orders should be imposed. The Court of Justice shall weigh the interests which it holds against each other and shall consider the freedom of information and expression, which is a matter of serious concern. Both the Internet and the current web site should be allowed to comment before a decision is made. The authorisation to grant the type of order must be used with great care and the most serious cases must be reserved. This means that no obstacles can be imposed in the access to legal material made available in violation of Grundlova § 100. The respiratory system shall balance various aspects of care. On the one hand, it protects the author's basic need to live off his work while encouraging creative activity. On the other hand, society and users will be promised their interest in access to nature while enforcing copyright on the Internet and other important concerns such as privacy, legal certainty and freedom of expression. We have placed great emphasis on finding balanced solutions to address these concerns, and this means, among other things, that the courts should weigh the different interests that they agree with each other in assessing whether action should be taken. I have great confidence that our courts will conduct good judgment when this is to be counterbalanced with other important considerations. Finally, the left says there is something in 2008 about the whole proposal. I want to say there's something a little bit 1984 over the whole argument to the left.

The reason why things have gone so quickly and painlessly, I believe, as the case-rapporteur, is that the theme has been thoroughly debated in the Children's Grand Notice on the run, in the setting of this message, which was debated and voted on in the Stortinget just before Christmas, in the media, at many conferences, and in connection with many asylum-children's actions last year and this year. When we know at the same time that the major evaluation of the message is under way and will be presented in about a month and a half, it is enough, understandably, many waiting to take up the asylum child-battery again in its full breadth. The starting point today is a Document 8 proposal, a proposal for a representative, on the best interests of children in the field of asylum policy, which proposes the following: firstly, that the government be asked to amend the foreign regulation to make it clear that the best interests of the child need to be given greater emphasis in terms of immigration-regulating considerations; secondly, that the government asks the government to clarify how the assessment of the child's best interests should be carried out; thirdly, that the Storting asks the government to assess whether the content of the child's best assessment is in line with international and national obligations; and finally, that the Stortinget asks the government to carry out a review of the current practice in asylum matters involving children. The proposers share, as they write, the unisony feedback from the hearing bodies that the Storing Communication contributes to further confusion through vague and partly contradictory statements rather than clarifying immigration regulatory considerations, and that it does not specify how the best judgements of the child should be carried out. The proposers therefore support the recommendation made by a large majority of the hearing bodies. The proposers explain why they believe that the ‘best’ is the primary consideration. The proposers believe that a legal review of the relationship between the provisions of the Foreign Affairs Act and the United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child and the practice of existing legislation against the international obligations arising from the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child could contribute to important clarifications. The proposers finally said that the government and the bulk of the population thought that there was going to be a policy change as a result of that message. It does not seem to have turned on, nor will it turn on in the future. To the committee reading: Having received a letter from the Grete Faremo Council attached to the matter here, the Committee collectively states that it supports the wish of the proposers for an asylum policy which is fair, legal and humane. Furthermore, the majority of the Committee, all except the member of the Christian People's Party, says that this message promotes a number of measures to ensure a fairer, more judicial and humane treatment of asylum-seeking children and their families, and it is presented in the vote. As far as the best interests of the child are concerned, the majority emphasises that this should be a fundamental consideration when considering residence for reasons of high human interest, as laid down by the Regulation. the foreign policy § 8-5. The majority also point to the fact that the recent UNE practice suggests that in some cases more emphasis is placed on immigration regulatory considerations than was foreseen at the time of the adoption of the legislation, while practices appear to be less unambiguous. The majority also gives its assessment of the above-mentioned Supreme Court rulings, which are also highlighted in the representative proposal, and also mentions Oslo's handling of the so-called Nathan case and UNE's in-depth reporting of November last year on the kind of practice we have had since last year. The majority believe – contrary to what the proposers claim – that there is no basis for concluding that the message does not affect practice. One must await the June evaluation and then decide on the extent to which a change in practice has taken place and whether further action is required to ensure that practices are in line with the intentions of the legislator. Right-wing members have their own comitology where this is well discussed, but where the conclusion is that current practice, where the best interests of children are to be assessed individually as well as against immigration-regulating considerations, should continue to be the policy in force. The member of the Committee from the Christian People's Party uses this setting to elaborate and argue for the draft representative and to promote its own proposal in line with that. It will probably be presented and commented on later today. I'm putting forward the committee's recommendation. Furthermore, the Committee has no comments, refers to the draft representative and urges the Stortinget to adopt a decision requiring the minutes to be attached.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e1683	I would also like to start by thanking the rapporteur, who has presented the broad majority of the Committee on this matter in an excellent way. This broad majority has underlined the importance of children’s interest in the handling of asylum applications through the high-quality child-growth communication. The right considers that the core of this issue lies in the understanding of the third paragraph of Section 38 of the Foreign Affairs Act, which explicitly indicates that the best interests of the child are fundamental, and states that even if the situation is not serious, children may be granted a residence permit even if the situation would otherwise provide the basis for an adult’s residence permit. The Convention on the Rights of the Child does not impose an absolute obligation on each case to decide. There is room for opposing considerations, such as: consideration of controlled and regulated immigration, which may in principle be given equal or even greater weight to the best interests of the child. The more important the decision is for the child’s situation, the more important it is to focus on the opposite considerations, such as: the immigration regulators. The Directorate for Foreign Affairs and the Department for Foreign Affairs must therefore carry out an overall assessment of the individual case for all reasons of humanitarian residence. The right is committed to caring for the interests of children in asylum matters. Therefore, the Right considers that it was important and appropriate to amend the Foreign Affairs Act of 2010 in order to ensure that the interests of children are respected in line with the requirements of the Convention on the Rights of the Child. At the same time, it is important to manage the institution in a good manner, so that people with a need for protection stay, while those who do not have this need are refused and have to return as quickly as practicable. The assessment of the best interests of the child in a case can be a challenge. It cannot be assumed that it will always be in the best interests of the child to have a permanent stay in Norway. In addition, the best interests of the child shall also be weighed against other important considerations, such as immigration regulatory considerations and the best interests of other children, in the sense that practices should not encourage more children to seek asylum in Norway if they do not need such protection. In fact, there is a balance between the interests of several children, where society and those not yet immigrated, those who do not remain on reception, have a legitimate need for protection. Today's practice, where the best of each child is to be assessed individually as well as against opposing considerations, should continue to apply to our immigration policy. At the same time, it is important that work on setting up return agreements needs to be intensified, so that children of asylum seekers with rejection do not remain in Norway for several years. The right will also actively use the aid policy to support the return of persons without legal residence in Norway, and allow the government to instruct UNE in the same way as the UDI, so that it has the opportunity to intervene in key issues and monitor practices as they follow. ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e1700	The attitude and debate so far show that the Stortinget is united on a very central premise: asylum policy should be fair, law-abiding and humane. This is fundamental to the Center Party as well. Any individual seeking asylum in Norway, or staying on other grounds, shall have the security of legal certainty and human rights. The issue of the best of children in asylum policy has been debated in depth in the year since the government issued the child-removal report – and before. It's not for no reason. Asylum policy is a complex policy area, with difficult dilemmas between the consideration of individuals in vulnerable situations and the consideration of a consistent, coherent and fair policy. Extra important and difficult are these balances when it comes to young people, whether they come alone or with family. Regardless of the background, children’s migrants will be in a particularly vulnerable situation — and therefore extra attention should be paid to these issues. This applies from the time of the search process and until the child may have been allowed to stay and be integrated into the Norwegian community. Additional consideration must also be given to children in cases where they are the subject of a refusal and shall return to – or together with – family or other caregivers. In recent years, the proportion of young people seeking protection in Norway is under 30pst. of the total number of asylum seekers. For various reasons, too many of these children and their families have been waiting for too long in a situation that they experience as unsolved. Suffering from this in many cases is due to the failure of parents to comply after final rejection, in any case it would be a burden for young people to have their childhood put on hold. The reception of asylum is not a good framework for a child's growth. In the public debate on refugee children, the focus is particularly on long-term young people. The interpretation of the UN Children’s Convention that the best interests of young people should be a fundamental consideration in all kinds of matters relating to young people is central and has been discussed. The Government’s recent practice in dealing with complaints in the field of foreign affairs suggested that in some cases more attention was given to immigration policy considerations than was foreseen at the time of the adoption of the legislation, and that practices at the same time appeared to be less unambiguous. As a result, part of the elements to be included in the assessment of the best interests of children were made clear. This applies, inter alia, to: age, total length of residence in Norway, whether the child has attended school or kindergarten, chatter Norwegian, takes part in leisure activities, has friends or otherwise is particularly related to the Norwegian community. Another important clarification was that both residence periods such as asylum seekers and residence periods after final rejection should be counted. These were important clarifications, but, above all, it is important to prevent young people from getting into such a situation that they remain for a long time in an unsolved situation. The message therefore focused on shorter processing times, faster return and faster residence of those who are allowed to stay. Proposals now seek to amend the foreign policy in order to make it clear that the best interests of the child need to be given greater emphasis. We believe that the Storing Message has just contributed to such clarification. In addition, the government will follow developments in practice in the future and UNE has been asked to explain its practice in such cases by 14 June of this year, which has already been mentioned. The parties argue that, in the light of this report, the government will decide whether further action is required to ensure that practices are in line with the intentions of the legislator.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e1866	There is broad political agreement on asylum and immigration policy in Norway. This is also reflected in the Committee's consideration. The exposure of these children has been linked to the life situation of the children and the fact that some of them do not know any country other than Norway. The requirement that the best interests of children should be assessed and strongly counted in the decision is correct. There shall be an individual assessment of each applicant. However, the ‘best of children’ criterion cannot override all other assessments. Children are part of a family and overall assessments must be made. The children have come together with their parents or are born in Norway, as appropriate, by parents who have been refused their asylum application. Thus, the case of children cannot be treated as if the children did not belong to a family. The government has taken the asylum children's case very seriously. The matter has been thoroughly addressed in the Children's paper on refugees. Steps have been taken to speed up the process. Individual treatment is conducted where the best weights of children are heavy, but where the overall weight is also assessed. We have a proper handling of these matters in Norway. It gives – along with following Norwegian and international laws – a policy that is predictable and fair in this field.

What I think this is about is whether or not the best interests of the child should be a fundamental consideration – when this is implemented in the Foreign Affairs Act – or not. The Norwegian Centre for Human Rights shows, in its consultation exercise to Children, that Article 3 states that, on all occasions involving children, the best interests of the child shall be a fundamental consideration or a primary consideration, as stated in the official English version. It must mean that the best interests of the child must be given weight, as opposed to weight. The wording also says something about the weight to be taken into account. The best interests of children are not just one of several considerations, but must be the primary consideration. I understand that, as a general rule, primary consideration should be given to other considerations and that exemptions must be justified separately. In order to ensure that we are given an emphasis for different reasons and that this is done in accordance with the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child, it is necessary that the elements included in the assessment are set out in the individual decisions. If the best interests of children are not realised in the balance against other reasons, the Christian People's Party believes that there must be at least justification, and that it must be made clear in the decision. This is what we believe we need in place to ensure the legal certainty of the perhaps most vulnerable children in the country – the asylum-seekers. It is incomprehensible to me that this is not in place, and I agree with the rapporteur, Aksel Hagen, that it is sad that further confusion is now being created. But it is – with respect to reporting – the government that is contributing today.

Competition promotes increased efficiency, and that is, after all – I note it – a broad consensus in the UK on competition in a number of areas, because there is no government that has proposed to abolish competition in large areas of society where competition has been introduced. In addition, DNA analysis in this area is a significant residue, and the fact that such analyses take a disproportionately long time weakens the progress of important criminal cases. This is a very important point in view of the proposal that has been put forward. Last year the NRC conducted a survey among a number of police districts and asked whether the police were satisfied with the time it took to get answers to these analyses, and whether the waiting time as of the day was acceptable. ‘We believe that it takes much too long — both in serious and less serious cases’ — was the message from one district. ‘ Answers take too long and cause unnecessary proceedings with the police’, says one head of another police district. A number of aid lawyers and defenders contacted the NRK at that time, saying that the waiting time for bioassay results was still a bottleneck in assault cases. The government claims – and will probably say later in the debate – that the state of things is relatively good, everything has become so much better. But those of us who travel out a little bit and get feedback from those who actually depend on this system, probably don't realize that the government's description of reality is correct. At least it's not perceived as a reality description out there. There are still significant residues in this system. ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e2256	Today, we are dealing with two legislative proposals – one in which the government proposes to introduce electronic control as a precautionary measure, and two minor adjustments to the conditions for the use of electronic control in criminal proceedings. As early as 2006, the government had consulted on a proposal to introduce electronic control as a precautionary measure. At that time, the government abandoned the proposal, both on the basis of consultation opinions and that there was no experience of electronic control as a system. The government introduced electronic control as an alternative form of settlement in 2008. The scheme, also called foot-link soning, started as a pilot project to gain experience in this new form of reconciliation. The scheme has been gradually expanded and now comprises eleven counties with a total of 220 places. Now, in May, the scheme is being extended to a further state, namely Northern Ireland. The evaluation of electronic control shows, inter alia: that relapses to new crime after an end of settlement are low. One of the reasons for this is that the convicted person can maintain work, school or other activation during the settlement. Maintaining family relationships is also important. This we know makes life easier to resume after settlement, and the likelihood of a relapse to new crime is reduced. This is how electronic control has become a punishment that works, and is fully in line with the big message with the same name, namely the penalty that works. It also works well in the selection of technological solutions. These experiences have provided the basis for testing electronic control as a protective surrogate. Norway has relatively high figures for the use of custody prisons. Norway has been repeatedly criticised by various international and regional agencies for the widespread use of custody prisons. There is therefore reason to consider whether the purpose of an attack or detention can be achieved by surrogates that are less invasive than prison. The proposal for electronic control as a precautionary measure may contribute to the reduction of the use of custody prisons and thus comply with our international Convention obligations. There is also reason to believe that it will release prison grounds for other purposes. The basic prerequisites for the Workers' Party to agree to electronic compensation as a caretaker's surrogate are that the measure is not at the expense of clarifying criminal acts and society's need for protection and security. Very many countries use electronic control in the implementation of penalties. Some of these have also introduced the system as a protective surrogate. These include England, Wales, Austria, the Netherlands and Portugal. The Ministry has assessed that electronic control will be particularly relevant as a precautionary measure in the event of a risk of new criminal offences, and in particular to young offenders. It is difficult to calculate the number of custody prisoners that can be in the target group for electronic control. Experience from Portugal shows that about 20 pst. by all custody prisons are transferred to electronic control. Directly transferred to Norwegian conditions will amount to about 200 persons. The Ministry assumes that the real figure in Norway will be lower and believes that the scheme may be valid for about 50 people at any time. According to the Ministry, the implementation of the care surrogate can be largely coordinated with the already established system of electronic control penalties. The working party believes that the experience and attitudes of electronic control in the implementation of penalties provide a good basis for taking a step forward and testing out electronic control also as a protective surrogate. We therefore support the proposal by the government. We also support the proposal for the adjustment to the conditions for the use of electronic control in criminal proceedings, by replacing the term ‘probation’ with ‘release’ and the clarification that the unconditional prison sentence should not exceed four months.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e2348	In the bill, the government is in favour of electronic control as an alternative to custody. There have been a high number of arrests throughout the country, while both high-security and low-security prisons have had almost full coverage. Several hearing bodies were sceptical or opposed to the use of electronic control as an alternative to custody when the proposal was being consulted in 2006. This was true of the Riksadvokaten, the state attorneys Agder, Hordaland, Vestfold and Telemark, and the criminal care regions south and north. In particular, the hearing bodies pointed out that the proposal did not serve the purpose of the custody prison. Other hearing bodies such as the Court of Justice, the Norwegian Judicial Association, the Criminal Office of the South-West Region and Legal Advice for Women supported the proposal. Very many countries both inside and outside Europe are currently using electronic control as part of criminal justice. Most people use electronic control during the criminal execution, either as an alternative to a shorter prison sentence in its entirety or as custodial measures in the case of longer prison sentences. Electronic control as an alternative to custody is less widespread. England and Wales, Austria, the Netherlands and Portugal are countries in Europe that use electronic control during the pre-trial period. In the United States, this type of use of electronic control is also common. Also for care, both follow-up, control and length of time will vary between countries. While it is difficult to estimate the number of custody prisoners that may be in the target population, the Ministry of Justice and Defence assumes that, at an estimated full capacity, electronic control as an alternative to custody will amount to about 50 seats with an estimated annual operating cost of just over 15 million. kr. The implementation will be established in those countries where the implementation of penalties has already been established by electronic means. The further establishment will follow the possible extension of criminal enforcement with electronic control. Since electronic control as an alternative to custody is not assumed to result in increased use of custody, but is to be a real alternative to imprisonment, the proposal will also, equally, reduce the need for the creation of new prison capacity. Operation of an equivalent number of high-security prisons costs around 40 million. kr per year. However, due to today’s lack of capacity in criminal services, prison sites cannot be closed down. ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e2524	One of my great political role models is William Wilberforce. He was elected to the British Parliament at the end of the 1700s. At that time he was at the beginning of the 20s – which is otherwise a perfect age for being elected to Parliament. From 1787 there was one thing for which he fought successfully – he fought for human dignity. That's why he fought slavery. He fought the slave trade and slave teams in British colonies. His starting point was that all people have the same value. In his time it was the fight against slavery that occupied him most. The United Kingdom was then a major act, and he was countered and defeated when he proposed the ban in Parliament every year. Much of the background was enough that enormous wealth was linked to just the slave trade. In 1807, Wilberforce was able to win with the first decision, and the slave trade was banned. Three days before William Wilberforce died, he experienced that slavery was abolished for good. In a sense, Norway has quite proud traditions of slavery. Denmark-Norway was the first country in the world to promote a law to ban slave trade. We say that slavery was abolished almost 200 years ago in Europe. Unfortunately, it's not like that. There are today more slaves in the world than the 10-12 million transported with the transatlantic slave trade. According to the international organization Free the Slaves, a total of 27 million people live as slaves today. We can certainly say that this is about huge sums of money. It will be in line with drug and arms trafficking – money that is certainly also used to fight, for example. policy changes that will increase efforts to combat trafficking in human beings. Trafficking in human beings is the slavery of our time. That is why we need to do everything we can to fight this – both nationally and internationally – in order to prevent the abuse and degradation of women, children or men. The Christian People's Party wants Norway to go to the brink of modern slavery. We have called for Norway to have a goal of becoming the world's first slave-free country. Today, human trafficking and slavery are more brutal and more widespread, but less visible. I therefore hope the Council of State will respond to the challenge given: what can we do to combat modern slavery? The Palermo Protocol, which entered into force at the end of 2003, contributed to a common international understanding of trafficking in human beings. It was in many ways an important step towards getting a step closer to the end of slavery. But a definition is far from enough, which also later has shown. The definition is interpreted very differently in different countries, which in turn leads to a different approach to combating slavery. It's unfortunate. If we're really going to do something about this, we need a common international effort. Slavery is the exploitation of children, women and men to perform work and services — including prostitution, profitability, drug sales and begging — through the use of violence, threats and other control mechanisms. People are treated as goods that can be bought and sold, and then put into work. Under Section 224 of the Criminal Code, trafficking in people is prohibited in Norway. In order to combat this injustice experienced by many people, innovation and transparency are important. The Bondevik II government presented the first action plan on trafficking in human beings about ten years ago, and since then, the Norwegian authorities have continuously assessed and adjusted the measures taken to counter trafficking in human beings and to assist victims. Unfortunately, we still have a long way to go — both nationally and internationally. The issues relating to trafficking in human beings are very complex, and we therefore need more knowledge to help the individual. Research in the field must therefore be given priority. In 2008, the Stortinget decided to introduce a ban on the purchase of sexual intercourse or action, which entered into force on 1 January 2009. The background for the introduction of the ban was, among other things: that there should be a measure to combat modern slavery. The introduction of the law was an important milestone in highlighting human dignity, but it cannot be done simply by a legislative change, it must be complemented by good social measures to get people out of prostitution. It has always been the intention of the Christian People's Party, but unfortunately the government has not succeeded well enough here. The opponents of the law claim that the law does not work because too few people have been prosecuted. There is a very unfair line of argument against the police. We cannot demand that they enforce laws unless we enable them to do so. I was just in Sweden, where all parties stand together on the sex purchase law and say that it is a very good law, as well as that the police and volunteers working with the prostitutes support the law. They have achieved good results, but it would not have happened if it had not been political will – something that unfortunately has been lacking in the red-greens. What they also seem to be achieving in Sweden, which I believe is crucial, is that in addition to society's asserting that it's not okay to buy sex, it's also become a widespread understanding that buying sex helps to maintain the demand for prostitutes and thus also slavery. The 2002 sexual habit survey shows that 13 pst. Norwegian men buy sexual services. We must therefore work to give our children and young people good attitudes, and in school we must not only learn about the transatlantic slave trade, but also modern slavery must be the theme. We know that poverty makes many people feel forced to sell their kids to slavery or think they're being offered the dream job abroad, then being sold on the street. The provision of assistance to the prostitutes must therefore be seen in this context. Slavery victims are in a very vulnerable situation. They are often entirely alone and have been subjected to serious violence, threats, abuse or other improper behaviour. In addition, their families in their home country may be under threat from representatives from the rear. All of this makes it hard for them to get out of slavery. The offer given must therefore be complete, so that it can actually result in the individual being freed from his backbone. Finally, to eradicate trafficking in human beings, there is a need to do something about the causes, and we need to intensify the fight against the international networks that sell people. Many prostitutes stay in Norway for a short or longer period of time and then move on to other countries. If we are to succeed in preventing the recruitment of prostitutes and the fight against slavery, we must work actively internationally. In a 2009 report from Norad, Unsecured Traffic, the international trends in trafficking in human beings were mapped. It states that the limitation of the demand for prostitution is an important measure to combat trafficking in human beings. They refer in the report to the organisation Coalition Against Trafficking in Women, CATW, which has pointed out that Swedish criminalisation of the purchase of sexual services has led to a significant decrease in victims of trafficking in human beings from Eastern Europe – that is good. Einstein once said that the definition of madness is to do what you've always done, and believe it will give other results. In the report from Norad, there are many examples of actions that can be implemented, and if we just do what we have always done, we get the same results we have always received – in this case, unfortunately, lack of results. Ergo, we also have to be willing to think new. It is therefore interesting to hear what the Council of State thinks about any new measures in the fight against modern slavery.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e2556	When the Sex Purchases Act was introduced, it had several purposes.
