ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e2632	I would first like to thank you for the three interpellations. The raising of three interpellations on the same theme illustrates the importance of continuing to focus on what is needed to combat trafficking in human beings and to help people out of prostitution. Interpellants have raised questions related both to the impact of our national legislation on prostitution and trafficking and to our international efforts in this area. It is natural for me first to say a few things about the situation in Norway. Today's criminal law has several provisions that address various acts relating to the purchase and sale of sexual services. These provisions aim, on the one hand, at preventing the purchase and sale of sexual services and, on the other, at preventing anyone from exploiting others for such activities and, on the other hand, at preventing the dissemination of prostitution. The penal code § 202 is often referred to as the Halik paragraph, and it affects the one promoting the prostitution of others. Intermediate activities and the organisation of sex clubs are examples of actions affected by the provision. It also affects the owner of premises and understands that the premises are to be used for prostitution. Section 202 of the Criminal Act also targets the person who, in the public notice, offers, communicates or asks for prostitution. The Criminal Act § 202a affects the purchase of sexual services from adults. It's this paragraph that's called the Sex Purchases Act. The aim of this provision was to contribute to the reduction of trafficking in human beings in Norway and to change attitudes towards the purchase of sexual services. The penal code § 224 applies to trafficking in human beings and is not just aimed at the exploitation of people in prostitution. Nevertheless, trafficking in human beings in Norway has been largely linked to the exploitation of women in prostitution. Otherwise, let me briefly remind you that the Stortinget, when we adopted our new penal code, continued the content of today's legislation. The law rests on the idea that prostitution is negative. It's harmful for a person to be in prostitution, and it's usually people in vulnerable and unhappy situations that end up in prostitution. Furthermore, there is a clear experience that organised prostitution often comes out of, and gives rise to, criminal activities of different kinds. Our social assistance measures have the task of assisting people in prostitution by helping them in their everyday lives, but also by encouraging and supporting them in attempts to leave their businesses. We also support attempts to reach out to customers and offer advice to get them to refrain from buying sexual services. We all see that legislation can be criticised for containing contradictions. Legislation allows the sale of sexual services, but punishes the purchase of the same services. Individuals may sell sexual services but are not allowed to organise activities in an appropriate manner. We must live with such paradoxes, and it is not the country in the world that has succeeded in reconciling all the considerations that should be taken care of with regard to the sale of sexual services in its legislation and enforcement practices. We are all talking, including me, about the fight against trafficking in human beings and prostitution. However, it is important to remember the nuances of the legislation that make it unprescious to say that we are combating prostitution, since such a use of terms may raise doubts about our solidarity and support for those in prostitution. Without a conviction that they need our support over punishment, the only natural thing would be to also ban the sale of sexual services. It's different with human trafficking. This modern form of slavery is a cynical exploitation of vulnerability and poverty and a serious form of profit-based crime. There are questions about how I think these legal provisions work to reduce the Norwegian prostitution market. Here I would first like to remind you that we continuously assess whether the law is up to date. Penalties must always reflect changes in the way prostitution is done, for example. how to establish contact between the customer and the seller. As a result of an enquiry from the Oslo police district, which has been targeted for several years to combat insidious activities and trafficking in human beings, the Ministry of Justice and Defence has seen the need to amend the penal code § 202 and will, in our opinion, be consulted on a proposal that will clearly affect all types of dissemination of prostitution. Recent case-law relating to advertising via the Internet has shown that the legislation has not been up-to-date. I would like to emphasise that our criminal provisions in this area are interrelated and that the assessment of their functioning and enforcement is therefore complicated. When you banned the purchase of sexual services, we were concerned about how the ban would work for women in prostitution. Would they become more vulnerable to violence and become less accessible to relief measures? We decided that after some time we would evaluate the effect of the ban. This evaluation is being carried out this year, but we already see major challenges in isolating the impact of the ban from other events. However, an evaluation is far more than counting persons in prostitution at different venues before and after the ban. The increase in trafficking in human beings has made it necessary for the police to combat organised prostitution in various ways. Even before the ban on the purchase of sexual services came into force, the police in Oslo had put in place a strategy to prevent foreign insidious networks from establishing themselves in the city. By means of an operation without a home, the police seek out homeowners who hire prostitutes and inform them that if they wish to avoid criminal prosecution, the rental relationship or prostitution must cease. Often, women are thrown out of their apartments on short notice. This strategy probably affects the situation of these women and their relationship with the police to a far greater extent than the fact that their customers are at risk of punishment. There is reason to believe that women in prostitution are failing to report that they have been subjected to violence or threats to the police, in fear that this could lead to their being thrown out of the apartment in which they live. We see here an unfortunate consequence of the paradoxes we encounter in the field of prostitution, and these are issues that we need to address. Our action plan against trafficking in human beings is a central governance document for this work. The Ministry collects information on the implementation of the action plan’s actions and on the enforcement of the law on prostitution and trafficking in human beings in many different ways. We receive information through meeting activities and through reporting by the police, other authorities, organisations and researchers. Overall, the impression is left that the police are giving the necessary priority to the task and that good results are being achieved. As for the international effort, let me say a little bit more before I go into it. Whether the police give the necessary priority to the task is a question of both quality and scope, whether sufficient capacity is set for this task, and also how the tasks associated with this area relate to other forms of organised crime. This is a total of problems that we have increased attention to — including how to solve these problems better. As far as our international efforts are concerned, the Ministry of Justice and Defence has the primary responsibility to coordinate the government's efforts against trafficking in human beings. Several ministries work together to meet the government's objectives. Our international efforts are managed by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, in close cooperation with the Ministry of Justice and Defence and other departments. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs asked Norad in 2008 to review efforts to combat trafficking in human beings. The main objective was to gain knowledge of the results and experiences of the Norwegian-funded effort and to see it in a wider international context. The result of the review was identified in three different reports — unsafe traffic is one of them. The reports form an important part of the background dossier of the Action Plan against Trafficking in Human Beings, presented by the Ministry of Justice and Security in December 2010, and valid until and including next year. A key consideration in Insecure traffic is that one of the main challenges in work is the complexity and need for an overall approach. Our action plan makes it clear that the government shares this view and therefore continues our broad international efforts, both through efforts to strengthen the international framework and through project support for prevention and assistance for victims. Insecure traffic sums up experience from work against trafficking in human beings and refers to some measures that are considered as ‘best practice’. I will give some examples of how the government has assessed and followed up the report. The report shows, as many have already mentioned, the Palermo Protocol on the prevention, control and punishment of trafficking in human beings, in particular women and children. This Protocol to the UN Convention on Transboundary Organised Crime, UNTOC, is the main global instrument against trafficking in human beings. The report points out that, despite the overall strategic approach of the Protocol to combating trafficking in human beings, the implementation of measures has proved to be very uneven. The Government agrees with this assessment. This is why Norway, within the UN, has actively supported the efforts to establish a monitoring mechanism for the UNTOC and the Palermo Protocol. It was expected that the Conference of the Parties to UNTOC would adopt the mechanism last October. However, there was no consensus due to differing views on funding and civil society participation. The matter is expected to be put on the agenda again at the next Conference of the Parties in 2014. Another action the report refers to is the need for the police to conduct economic investigations into human trafficking issues in order to follow the flows of money both before and after attacks. I believe this – like the inter-pellant Marthinsen – is an important and correct measure. Action 2 in our Action Plan states that Norway should contribute to international efforts to stop illegal capital flows from trafficking in human beings and strengthen financial investigations for the purpose of withdrawing profits. Norway has therefore funded a United Nations study on money from criminal activities, including trafficking in human beings. The study was the basis for a subsequent resolution initiated by Norway and adopted by the United Nations Commission on Crime and the General Assembly. In turn, the Norwegian police have carried out investigations that have resulted in judgments on money laundering arising from trafficking in human beings and organised prostitution. «Insecure traffic» recommends raising awareness of the fact that men are also victims of trafficking in human beings and that efforts to combat forced labour are given sufficient priority. Action 1 of our Action Plan mentions that Norway, through participation in the Baltic Sea Council, will strengthen the focus on trafficking in human beings on the labour market. Norway, at the Ministry of Justice and Defence, chaired the Baltic Sea Council working group against trafficking in human beings for one year, until July 2011. The working group is now implementing a project in the Baltic Sea region to strengthen cooperation against forced labour at national and international level. As part of this project, a national seminar on forced labour was held in Oslo in mid-March this year. This is one example of our national efforts to combat human trafficking being integrated into our international commitment in the field. We have ensured that our national experts are up-to-date on international experience, and in a similar way, Norwegian-based cooperation and exchange of experience between countries and organisations are being helped. The government is making a broad and comprehensive effort to strengthen the fight against trafficking in human beings internationally — efforts that support the objective of preventing trafficking in human beings in Norway and of preventing and combating this crime in other countries as well. However, we must constantly focus on what we can do better, both in terms of legislation, concrete action and enforcement.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e2697	I would like to thank the inter-pellants for putting human trafficking on the agenda of today's Stortinget. Trafficking in human beings is a cross-border organised crime and a serious violation of human rights. It is a form of crime that strikes the victims very hard and puts the international community to the test. Trafficking in human beings violates fundamental human rights, including the prohibition of slavery and forced labour under the European Convention on Human Rights. It is therefore entirely in place that the interpellant Rupstad of the Christian People's Party calls human trafficking the slavery of our time. In Norway, trafficking in human beings is mainly trafficking in women and minors exploited for prostitution or other sexual purposes, but there is also trade in which men are victims. False promises of a better life lead the victims of cross-border trafficking in human beings, and many believe that they should work in the service professions, as domestic workers or as models. Trafficking in human beings is when one person exploits another for prostitution or other sexual purposes, for forced labour or for forced services. There may be begging, drug sales along Akerselva or organ harvesting — this through coercion, violence, threats or abuse of the person’s particularly vulnerable situation. Some of the victims know they're going into the sex industry, but they don't know they're going to get paid little or nothing. Nor do they know that threats, violence or withdrawal of freedom of movement or right of access to their own bodies can become part of everyday life. Many are beaten, raped, imprisoned and starved until they do what the rearmen demand. I would particularly like to use the opportunity here today to bring forward two important projects offering assistance and support to victims of trafficking in human beings. The ROSSA project has been at the heart of the assistance with its offer of follow-up to victims since 2005. ROSA offers assistance and protection throughout Norway 24 hours a day for women exposed to trafficking in human beings, and they do an invaluable job to assist these victims. The crisis centre in Bodø had for a long time a young woman who was exposed to human trafficking, living with her, and they have stressed emphatically that it would be extremely difficult for them to give the help and support that was needed to this woman, without having ROSA there as a contact point. Earlier this year I visited Laura's House, which is here in Oslo. It's a project directed by the Church's Bymition. They offer safe housing to victims of trafficking in human beings. In addition to housing, the project shall provide social follow-up related, inter alia, to: social, health and legal challenges. It is an offer that was made because many expressed concern that victims of trafficking were living in crisis centres for a long time. It was particularly worrying – and still is – when these victims become residents of children in crisis centres for several months and up to one year. Laura’s Hus is the only project in Norway that is doing this, and the government’s action plan refers to the project. It is important that this be followed up in the government's work on victims of trafficking in human beings in the future, and that consideration be given to making these two projects particularly permanent. Interpellant Nybakk highlighted the link between prostitution and trafficking in human beings in particular — as far as the other interpellants were concerned. I want to go ahead with the work done by the Trondheim police with the Sex Purchases Act. More than half of the illegal sex purchases that are exposed here in the country are revealed in Trondheim, and that of the South-Trøndelag police district’s own group, called ‘Pimp It’. There are two permanent officials assigned to only work on sex-purchase matters. They focus on outreach activities, they check that the prostitutes are doing well, they work to prevent street prostitution from occurring in the city, and they reveal hallucinations and trafficking in human beings. Knowledge in general in society is important. I liked the idea of the representative, Ropstad, to bring this into school. Knowledge of crisis centres is also important in order to be able to deal with this properly, but it is important that the police know how to carry out the special questioning of these victims in as gentle a manner as possible. Many of these women will have trouble trusting two great men to hear their story, once they have told their story to the police in other countries who have been involved with the backers. By talking to these policemen, their lives have become worse, and they have continued to live as sex slaves.

I believe that you should have put the whole package – not just with reflection time, but maybe with microcredit, maybe with knowledge building, which could give many of the women when they go back, the opportunity to get out of the criminal structures and have enough structure and control of their lives to enable them to get out of the sewer they're in. I notice that lately the state has also been convicted because we are unable to protect witnesses, women who have been subjected to violence, well enough. It is a warning that we are not good enough to protect witnesses or to prioritise this high enough. It was also shocking to see stories on Dagsrevyen about the women who stand up and actually want to testify against – I don't think 'dreite bag' is a parliamentary expression, so that's why: the worst traffickers. That you can't support those women who actually break out, who actually report to the police, who actually say they're willing to testify to end these criminal structures, means that we're not good enough. We have to make sure that the witnesses – the women who want to break out – are raised to the gold chair and do not feel that they are being pushed down and not taken seriously.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e2909	I am sure that the many examples of trafficking in human beings that are mentioned in the room today make an impression on more than me. We need to focus on new measures that can also strengthen efforts to combat trafficking in human beings and reduce prostitution. There are several who have asked how to assess Norwegian efforts and who have also mentioned the Council of Europe. It may be interesting to note that Norway has ratified the Council of Europe Convention on Action against Trafficking in Human Beings. We were also a driving force in the efforts to establish and operate the body of experts that bears the ringing name GRETA, which monitors the implementation of the Council of Europe Convention by the Member States. GRETA launched its first evaluation of Norway last year. The report on Norway is published on the Council of Europe websites this week and will provide good advice on the further development of Norwegian measures to combat trafficking in human beings. The report points out that Norway has taken a number of important steps to prevent and combat trafficking in human beings. It highlights in particular our action plans, cross-ministerial cooperation and the establishment of the Coordination Unit for Victims of Trafficking in Human Beings. Then GRETA also makes recommendations and addresses criticisms in different areas. Further training of all actors involved is recommended. In particular, the identification and follow-up of potential victims of trafficking in human beings should be prioritised in the training. The report also calls for a more active approach to the work against trafficking in human beings by children, including the Roma. Norway is boasted that the identification of victims of trafficking can be made by a wide range of actors, but there are no clearer procedures for such identification and a formalised national system for the organisation of assistance to victims. I mention these examples, because here we get good suggestions that we want to take with us in the further work, along with the input that has been made in the debate today. We also need to look at this in a broader perspective. There have been several examples of how we need to focus on the cash flows. Legal proposals relating to witness protection, which is right on the corner, as well as proposals for changes to the Mafia paragraph, have also been mentioned, and we have strengthened international police cooperation, where, with the Schengen Information System, version II, which aired on 9 April, we have a completely different and stronger system to combat frontier-free crime.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e2940	Work is the very foundation of our welfare model and the key to combating poverty and social distress. Overall, unemployment is low in Norway, as is the case in the other European countries, but for those with disabilities, the opposite is true. From January 2006 to January this year, the number of people with disabilities has increased from 153 to 211 to 190. In September 2006, there were 61,700 on measures, while in January 2013 there were only 55,703 people. This means that in 2006 they got approx. 40 pst. of those with reduced ability to work, this figure fell to about 26 pst in 2013. Some of the increase in the number of people without work is due to the removal of temporary unemployment, but the point is that there are fewer people getting help and waiting time increases. That's not good. The Christian People's Party has therefore invested money to increase its efforts in the last alternative budgets. More than a year ago, the Brofoss Committee delivered its NOU on labour-oriented measures. The Christian People's Party, together with the opposition, has asked the government to come to Stortinget with a big message following the NOU. We need a comprehensive overview of labour market supply and a political debate on the way forward. The government has not yet wanted this, as I see it, but it is uncertain why. State Secretary Norvald Mo said at the NHO trade conference on 20 March this year that the Council of State had signaled to Stortinget that she would come to Stortinget with a follow-up to matters and issues from the Brofoss Committee. But we in Stortinget don't know when or how this is going to happen. We want very strongly to get a complete follow-up. It's urgent, not least because we also have great challenges in waiting times. According to the management companies, it takes two to three years from being ill-reported until action is taken. It's not sustainable, especially when we know that early action is absolutely crucial. It should also be mentioned that the Riksrevision has highlighted major differences in the efficient use of resources in Nav. The potential for improvement is high. Cooperation between the public sector and the response apparatus needs to be improved. In this work, it is essential that Nav carry out good job performance assessments and disseminate the individual user for proper action. It is obvious that Nav has a job to do in terms of work performance assessments, as the evaluation from Proba also showed. The Brofoss Committee had a broad mandate, and there were great expectations of the work. The selection is divided into its setting. The main reason for this disagreement was the extent to which normal working life would be used in the measures, whether Nav would produce its own services or reinvent its order role, etc. The committee presented three different models for the future organisation of work and organisation of work. Model 1 is based on the competence and organisation of today's growth and return companies. Model 2 gives Nav more responsibility and is the most radical, by being based on the purchase of private players through increased outsourcing. It is also proposed that the actors should be able to make a profit. Model 3 is between models 1 and 2. Signals that Nav has entered into service or is in the process of using Model 2, which means the launch of competition and the risk of losing the competences that have been built up today, are very alarming. From the Christian People's Party we want to signal that model 1 in the Brofoss range seems to be the best solution. It addresses many of the challenges ahead of us. A lifting of the action thresholds will eliminate the long waiting periods. This will increase the flow rate, which will allow more people to get the help they need more quickly. In addition, we believe that Model 1 will ensure that the real needs of the individual user are met. It states that reintroduction is as close to normal working life as possible, but without forgetting the need for many participants to qualify. Just as a greater part of the re-duction should be done in close association with normal working life, the Brofoss Committee was very concerned. Today we see that this happens to a much greater extent than before. Labour market enterprises work more outlyingly, which may not be noticed — or discovered — enough, as the statistics are actually quite old, and that there is very little research available. It is also important to stress that model 1 serves those user groups that cannot work in normal working life. When the same action organiser is to follow up users to a successful outcome without delay, it is to a greater extent avoided that they become throwballs in the system. The user can then have a disability or a job arranged without unnecessary attempts at action or waiting between them. The main challenge with the other two models is that model 2 is strongly in favour of and model 3 opens up to, increased tendering and competitive outsourcing. Tenders may be good where there is a limited task that is measurable in dollars and ears what the effect will be. But working with people thinks I have to deal with something different. The Christian People's Party is skeptical about the use of bids and competitive outsourcing as a central management tool when we move within the area of the return field. The main reason for this, as we see it, is that the services that each individual needs must be individually adapted. This individual adaptation we think is going to be demanding within a system based on competitive outsourcing. People who need assistance to get back to work or permanent organisation are not a homogeneous group, rather the opposite. Of course, I should not be so categorical as to say that tenders are a poor solution when it comes to adapting services to the individual. But I think it will be extremely demanding to get to, and administratively demanding resource. Policy also shows from other fields that price can be of great importance, and the quality may be less. The evaluation of the use of tenders will have to address the question of whether it will provide better services, or equally good services, to more than today. As the president probably hears, I doubt it. In addition, tendering processes undermine the possibility of good and close cooperation between Nav and the action organiser, which has proved to be crucial to getting people back to work. There is a lot of good work going on in the labour market. The NHO project Rings in the water is a good example in this way. Attractive enterprises do a good job, and voluntary actors such as Frtex, the Church's Bymition and the Church's Social Service also make a very good contribution to achieving the objectives of inclusion in the labour market. Growth and investment are actors with a high level of experience and competence in the labour market, and they are therefore a very important speaker in the debate on Norway's labour market policy. I constantly meet people who are out of work and who want to contribute what they can. Recently, the Dagsrevyen showed a report where it was pointed out that half of those who have epilepsy are illiterate, but that many of them want to be at work. The willingness to work for those with reduced working capacity is great, and unfortunately the major obstacles are encountered on the road. Lack of knowledge by employers about the measures available to include them in the labour market may be one of them. Wrong organisation and poor use of the opportunities that are present by both the Nav and the authorities are also perhaps as major obstacles. How more people are going to fit into working life will be our big question in the future, both because it makes sense for individuals to be allowed to be part of the working life, and because we need labour in the future. We have to make sure that everyone who can and has the opportunity gets to be involved and contribute. To know more about the individual user and what will work for different people, we also need more research in the field. In addition, Nav has to require performance and efficiency to ensure that resources are used in the best possible way. Labour market policy is important, and my concern today is not necessarily to say that the government has few measures and does not do anything, but that we need to see a whole in this work. We hope that the Council of State today will respond, preferably, that she will return with a big message in this area. Only then can we have a thorough debate, and the Stortinget can point the way forward, which can also give predictability to all parties.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e3675	During the debate, the representative Dagrun Eriksen put forward four proposals on behalf of the Christian People's Party. Proposal No. 1 sounds: ‘The government is asking for a change in the foreign policy to make it clear that the best interests of the child need to be given greater emphasis in terms of immigration regulation, in accordance with Norway’s human rights obligations and otherwise in line with the intentions of the Foreign Act.’ Proposal No. 2 sounds: ‘The government asks the government to clarify how the best assessment of the child’s best performance is to be carried out.’ Proposal No. 3 sounds: ‘The government asks the government to assess whether the content of the child’s best estimate is in line with international and national commitments.’ Proposal No. 4 sounds: ‘The government is asked to review current practices in asylum cases involving children, where the practice of the provisions of the Foreign Affairs Act is viewed against the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child.’ The left has announced that they will support the proposals.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-04-29.ana.ud170e3719	During the debate, Ulf Leirstein put forward a proposal on behalf of the Progress Party and the Right. The proposal reads as follows: ‘The Government is asked immediately to facilitate the analysis of DNA by several accredited actors in criminal matters with a view to achieving increased legal certainty and quality in justice.’ The Christian People's Party and the Left have announced that they will support the proposal.
